0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle M^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle M^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}|}

(63)

where

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle v_{H}\hfil

is the heliocentric ecliptic velocity vector of the meteor,
\mu_{\astrosun}=$1.327\,124\,400\,18\text{\times}{10}^{11}\text{\,}{\mathrm{km}}^{3}\text{\,}{\mathrm{s}}^{-2}$
is the gravitational constant of the Sun, and

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle M^{\prime}\hfil

is the heliocentric ecliptic position vector of the meteoroid.

The semi-major axis in AU is:

a=\frac{-\mu_{\astrosun}}{2\epsilon r_{AU}}

(64)

where
r_{AU}=$149\,597\,870.7\text{\,}\mathrm{km}$
is one astronomical unit in kilometers. Mean motion in radians per day can be calculated as:

n=86400\sqrt{\frac{GM_{\astrosun}}{\left(1000|a|r_{AU}\right)^{3}}}

(65)

where
G=$6.673\,84\text{\times}{10}^{-11}\text{\,}{\mathrm{m}}^{3}\text{\,}{\mathrm{kg}}^{-1}\text{\,}{\mathrm{s}}^{-2}$
is the gravitational constant, and
M_{\astrosun}=$1.988\,55\text{\times}{10}^{30}\text{\,}\mathrm{kg}$
is the mass of the Sun. The orbital period in years is:

T=\frac{2\pi}{86400Y_{S}}\sqrt{\frac{(r_{AU}a)^{3}}{\mu_{\astrosun}}}

(66)

where
Y_{S}=365.256363004
is the sidereal year in days. Next, we calculate the orbital angular momentum vector:

(77)

and if
\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle M^{\prime}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle v_{H}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle v_{H}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle v_{H}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle v_{H}\hfil$\crcr}}}<0
then
\nu=2\pi-\nu
.

The eccentric anomaly
E
is:

E=\atantwo\left(\sqrt{1-e^{2}}\sin\nu,e+\cos\nu\right)

(78)

from which the mean anomaly
M
can be calculated as:

M=E-e\sin E

(79)

The time in days since the last perihelion passage, reference to
TDB
, is:

\Delta t_{\varpi}=\frac{Ma^{3/2}}{k}

(80)

where
k=0.01720209895(\textrm{AU})^{3/2}(\textrm{day})^{-1}(\textrm{solar mass})^{-1/2}
is the Gaussian gravitational constant.

Finally, we calculate the Tisserand parameter with respect to Jupiter as:

T_{J}=\frac{a_{J}}{a}+2\sqrt{(1-e^{2})\frac{a}{a_{J}}}\cos i

(81)

where
a_{J}=5.204267\mathrm{AU}
is the semi-major axis of Jupiter.

Appendix D
Earth-centered inertial coordinates

The Earth-centered inertial (ECI) coordinates are a Cartesian coordinate system where the X-Y plane coincides with the equatorial plane of the Earth, and the X axis passes through the equinox of the given epoch. The Z axis passes through the Earth’s North pole. As the coordinate system is permanently fixed to the celestial sphere, a fixed point on the surface of the Earth will have changing coordinates in time. As we assume that the observations are given in the epoch of date, we keep the ECI coordinates in the epoch of date as well.

Let the distance from the centre of the Earth to the position given by geographical coordinates in the WGS84 system be calculated as follows:

N=\frac{r_{e}}{\sqrt{1-e_{e}^{2}\sin^{2}\varphi}}

(82)

where
r_{e}
is the equatorial radius of the Earth as defined by the WGS84 system,
r_{e}=$6\,378\,137.0\text{\,}\mathrm{m}$
, and
e_{e}
is the equatorial ellipticity of an oblate Earth:

e_{e}=\sqrt{1-\frac{r_{p}^{2}}{r_{e}^{2}}}

(83)

where
r_{p}
is the polar radius of the Earth,
r_{p}=$6\,356\,752.314\,245\text{\,}\mathrm{m}$
. The polar ellipticity is:

e_{p}=\sqrt{\frac{r_{e}^{2}-r_{p}^{2}}{r_{p}^{2}}}

(84)

D.1
Converting geographical coordinates to ECI

Let
\varphi
be the geodetic latitude,
\lambda
the longitude,
h
the height above a WGS84 model for Earth, and
\theta^{\prime}
the apparent local sidereal time (LST). Note that the height is not the same as the Mean Sea Level (MSL) height reported by Google Earth and some GPS devices. If the MSL height is used, it has to be first converted to WGS84 height
(
Pavlis
et al. 2012
)
. The apparent LST
\theta^{\prime}
can be calculated using the procedure described in
Meeus 1998
page 88 and
Clark 2010
, see Appendix
E
for equations.

First, the coordinates are transformed into Earth-Centred Earth-Fixed (ECEF) coordinates:

\displaystyle x_{ECEF}=(N+h)\cos\varphi\cos\lambda

(85)

\displaystyle y_{ECEF}=(N+h)\cos\varphi\sin\lambda

\displaystyle z_{ECEF}=\left((1-e_{e}^{2})N+h\right)\sin\varphi

The radius of the Earth at the given geodetic latitude is then:

R_{h}=\sqrt{x_{ECEF}^{2}+y_{ECEF}^{2}+z_{ECEF}^{2}}

(86)

Using the geocentric latitude
\varphi^{\prime}
:

\varphi^{\prime}=\atantwo\left(z_{ECEF},\sqrt{x_{ECEF}^{2}+y_{ECEF}^{2}}\right)

(87)

The ECI coordinates in the epoch of date are then calculated as:

\displaystyle x_{ECI}=R_{h}\cos\varphi^{\prime}\cos\theta^{\prime}

(88)

\displaystyle y_{ECI}=R_{h}\cos\varphi^{\prime}\sin\theta^{\prime}

\displaystyle z_{ECI}=R_{h}\sin\varphi^{\prime}

D.2
ECI to geographical coordinates

Given the apparent sidereal time at Greenwich
\theta_{0}^{\prime}
(see equation
97
), the longitude can be calculated as:

\lambda=\atantwo\left(y_{ECI},x_{ECI}\right)-\theta_{0}^{\prime}

(89)

The geodetic latitude
\varphi
is calculated as:

\displaystyle p=\sqrt{x_{ECI}^{2}+y_{ECI}^{2}}

(90)

\displaystyle\vartheta=\atantwo\left(z_{ECI}r_{e},pr_{p}\right)

\displaystyle\varphi=\atantwo\left(z_{ECI}+e_{p}^{2}r_{p}\sin^{3}\vartheta,p-e_{e}^{2}r_{e}\cos^{3}\vartheta\right)

Care must be taken when calculating the height near exact poles due to numerical instabilities. If the coordinates are near the poles, and we take this as being within
1\text{\,}\mathrm{km}
from the poles, which can be determined by testing if both conditions
|x_{ECI}|<1000
and
|y_{ECI}|<1000
are true, the height is calculated as:

h=|z_{ECI}|-r_{p}

(91)

otherwise, the height above a WGS84 ellipsoid is calculated as:

\displaystyle N=\frac{r_{e}}{\sqrt{1-e_{e}^{2}\sin^{2}\varphi}}

(92)

\displaystyle h=\frac{p}{\cos\varphi}-N

This height is given in the WGS84 convention, if the height above mean sea level (MSL) is desired, a correction described in
Pavlis
et al. 2012
has to be applied.

Appendix E
Local apparent sidereal time

First, we calculate the nutation components
\Delta\psi
and
\Delta\epsilon
in equation
94
as given in
Meeus 1998
, chapter 22. We use the set of equations which give around
0.5\text{\,}\mathrm{\SIUnitSymbolArcsecond}
precision, which we deem sufficient for needs of meteoroid orbits. The dynamical time
TDB
is used.
\Omega
is the longitude of the ascending node of the Moon’s mean orbit on the ecliptic measured from the mean equinox of the date,
L
is the mean longitude of the Sun, and
L^{\prime}
is the mean longitude of the Moon. The values are in degrees.

\displaystyle T=\frac{TDB-2451545}{36525}

(93)

\displaystyle\Omega=125.04452-1934.136261T

\displaystyle L=280.4665+36000.7698T

\displaystyle L^{\prime}=218.3165+481267.8813T

The nutation in longitude
\Delta\psi
and the nutation in obliquity
\Delta\epsilon
are calculated in arc seconds as:

\displaystyle\Delta\psi=-17.2\sin\Omega-1.32\sin 2L-0.23\sin 2L^{\prime}+0.21\sin 2\Omega

(94)

\displaystyle\Delta\epsilon=9.2\cos\Omega+0.57\cos 2L+0.1\cos 2L^{\prime}-0.09\cos 2\Omega

Next, we calculate the mean sidereal time of the Earth (Greenwich Sidereal Time) in degrees. Note that the time used here is not dynamical.

\displaystyle t=\frac{JD-2451545}{36525}

(95)

\displaystyle\theta_{0}=280.46061837+360.98564736629(JD-2451545)

\displaystyle+0.000387933t^{2}-\frac{t^{3}}{38710000}

The mean obliquity of the Earth in arc seconds
\epsilon_{0}
is calculated using
U
, which is the time measured in units of 10000 Julian years from J2000 (note that the dynamical time is used):

\displaystyle U=\frac{TDB-2451545}{3652500}

(96)

\displaystyle\epsilon_{0}=84381.448-4680.93U

\displaystyle-1.55U^{2}

\displaystyle+1999.25U^{3}

\displaystyle-51.38U^{4}

\displaystyle-249.67U^{5}

\displaystyle-39.05U^{6}

\displaystyle+7.12U^{7}

\displaystyle+27.87U^{8}

\displaystyle+5.79U^{9}

\displaystyle+2.45U^{10}

The apparent sidereal time at Greenwich in degrees is calculated as:

\displaystyle\theta_{0}^{\prime}=\theta_{0}+\frac{\Delta\psi}{3600}\cos\frac{\epsilon_{0}+\Delta\epsilon}{3600}

(97)

After converting to radians, care must be taken to wrap the computed value inside the
[0,2\pi]
range using modulus operator:

\theta_{0}^{\prime}=\theta_{0}^{\prime}\bmod 2\pi

(98)

Finally, the apparent local sidereal time
\theta^{\prime}
can be calculated as:

\theta^{\prime}=(\theta_{0}^{\prime}+\lambda+2\pi)\bmod 2\pi

(99)

where
\lambda
is the geodetic longitude of the observer.

Appendix F
Horizontal to equatorial coordinate conversion

Right ascension
\alpha
and declination
\delta
are calculated from azimuth
A
, altitude
a
, Julian date
JD
, and geographical coordinates of the observer, longitude
\lambda
and latitude
\varphi
, as:

\displaystyle H=\atantwo\left(-\sin A,\tan a\cos\varphi-\cos A\sin\varphi\right)

(100)

\displaystyle\alpha=\theta^{\prime}-H

\displaystyle\delta=\arcsin\left(\sin\varphi\sin a+\cos\varphi\cos a\cos A\right)

where
H
is the local hour angle and
\theta^{\prime}
is the apparent local sidereal time (see Appendix
E
).

Appendix G
Equatorial to horizontal coordinate conversion

The azimuth
A
and altitude
a
are calculated from right ascension
\alpha
, declination
\delta
Julian date
JD
, and geographical coordinates of the observer, longitude
\lambda
and latitude
\varphi
, as:

\displaystyle H=\theta^{\prime}-\alpha

(101)

\displaystyle A=\pi+\atantwo\left(\sin H,\cos H\sin\varphi-\tan\delta\cos\varphi\right)

\displaystyle a=\arcsin\left(\sin\varphi\sin\delta+\cos\varphi\cos\delta\cos H\right)

where
H
is the local hour angle and
\theta^{\prime}
is the apparent local sidereal time (see
E
).

Appendix H
Precessing equatorial coordinates

We follow the rigorous method of
Meeus 1998
, pages 134 - 135, for precessing the right ascension
\alpha
and declination
\delta
from epoch
JD_{0}
to epoch
JD
. The beginning of each epoch is defined by their respective Julian dates. Please note that
\zeta
,
z
and
\theta
are given in degrees.

\displaystyle T=\frac{JD_{0}-2451545}{36525}

(102)

\displaystyle t=\frac{JD-JD_{0}}{36525}

\displaystyle\zeta=\frac{1}{3600}\left[(2306.2181+1.39656T-0.000139T^{2})t\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(0.30188-0.000344T)t^{2}+0.017998t^{3}\right]

\displaystyle z=\frac{1}{3600}\left[(2306.2181+1.39656T-0.000139T^{2})t\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(1.09468+0.000066T)t^{2}+0.018203t^{3}\right]

\displaystyle\theta=\frac{1}{3600}\left[(2004.3109-0.85330T-0.000217T^{2})t\right.

\displaystyle\left.-(0.42665+0.000217T)t^{2}-0.041833t^{3}\right]

\displaystyle A=\cos\delta\sin(\alpha+\zeta)

\displaystyle B=\cos\theta\cos\delta\cos(\alpha+\zeta)-\sin\theta\sin\delta

\displaystyle C=\sin\theta\cos\delta\cos(\alpha+\zeta)+\cos\theta\sin\delta

\displaystyle\alpha^{\prime}=\atantwo(A,B)+z

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}=\arcsin C

where
\alpha^{\prime}
and
\delta^{\prime}
are precessed coordinates. If the declination is close to the celestial poles (which we define this as less than
[formula]
from the poles), it is calculated differently due to numerical instabilities. If
($$-|\delta|)<$$
is true, the declination should be calculated as:

\delta^{\prime}=\arccos\sqrt{A^{2}+B^{2}}

(103)

Appendix I
Ecliptic coordinates

The geocentric right ascension
\alpha_{g}
and declination
\delta_{g}
at the given epoch (the epoch defined by a Julian date
JD
, usually at J2000, thus
JD
= 2451545) can be converted to geocentric ecliptic longitude
\lambda_{g}
and latitude
\beta_{g}
with the procedure described below. First, a precise obliquity of the Earth at the
JD
of the epoch has to be calculated;
\Delta\epsilon
can be calculated using equation
94
and the mean obliquity
\epsilon_{0}
using equation
96
. The true obliquity of the Earth in degrees is then simply:

\epsilon=\frac{\epsilon_{0}+\Delta\epsilon}{3600}

(104)

The ecliptic longitude and latitude are then:

\displaystyle\lambda_{g}=\atantwo\left(\sin\epsilon\sin\delta_{g}+\sin\alpha_{g}\cos\delta_{g}\cos\epsilon,\cos\alpha_{g}\cos\delta_{g}\right)

(105)

\displaystyle\beta_{g}=\arcsin\left(\cos\epsilon\sin\delta_{g}-\sin\alpha_{g}\cos\delta_{g}\sin\epsilon\right)
